We have a homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| [U/H](\mathbb {R}) \right| \cong H^1(G, H(\mathbb {C}))$$\end{document}[U/H](R)≅H1(G,H(C)) as spaces over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}U(R) by Proposition 4.9, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(G, H(\mathbb {C})) \rightarrow U(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}H1(G,H(C))→U(R) is a topological covering by Lemma 4.8. □ □

Smith–Thom for classifying stacks and groupoid cohomology

Smith–Thom for classifying stacks

Proof of Proposition 1.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}:=[\Gamma \rightrightarrows {pt}]$$\end{document}X:=[Γ⇉pt]. By Example 2.6, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {\mathscr {X}}^G \right| \simeq H^1(G,\Gamma )$$\end{document}XG≃H1(G,Γ), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(G, \Gamma )$$\end{document}H1(G,Γ) is the finite discrete space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z^1(G,\Gamma )/\sim $$\end{document}Z1(G,Γ)/∼ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z^1(G,\Gamma )\subset \Gamma $$\end{document}Z1(G,Γ)⊂Γ the set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Gamma $$\end{document}γ∈Γ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \sigma (\gamma )=e$$\end{document}γσ(γ)=e and ∼ ∼ the equivalence relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \sim \beta \gamma \sigma (\beta )^{-1}$$\end{document}γ∼βγσ(β)-1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in \Gamma $$\end{document}β∈Γ. Therefore, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dim H^*(\left| {\mathscr {X}}^G \right| ,\mathbb {Z}/2)=\# H^1(G,\Gamma ).$$\end{document}dimH∗(XG,Z/2)=#H1(G,Γ). Moreover, by Proposition 4.4, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \mathcal {I}_{{\mathscr {X}}} \right| \simeq \Gamma /\Gamma $$\end{document}IX≃Γ/Γ so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dim H^*(\left| \mathcal {I}_{{\mathscr {X}}} \right| , \mathbb {Z}/2)=\# (\Gamma /\Gamma ),$$\end{document}dimH∗(IX,Z/2)=#(Γ/Γ), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma /\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ/Γ is set of conjugacy classes of Γ Γ. So Proposition 1.8 follows from the following group theoretic lemma, whose proof has been suggested to us by Will Sawin. □ □

Lemma 5.1

Let Γ Γ be a finite group with an action of G. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#H^1(G,\Gamma ) \le \# (\Gamma /\Gamma ).$$\end{document}#H1(G,Γ)≤#(Γ/Γ).

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :\Gamma \rightarrow \Gamma $$\end{document}σ:Γ→Γ be the involution corresponding to the G-action. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \text {-conj}$$\end{document}σ-conj be the equivalence relation on Γ Γ induced by the action of Γ Γ on its self by σ σ-conjugacy (i.e. h acts by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(g)=hg\sigma (h^{-1})$$\end{document}h(g)=hgσ(h-1). For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \Gamma $$\end{document}h∈Γ, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Stab}_{\sigma }(h)$$\end{document}Stabσ(h) (resp. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[h]_{\sigma }$$\end{document}[h]σ) be the stabilizer (resp. the orbit) of h for the σ σ-conjugacy action and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Stab}(h)$$\end{document}Stab(h) (resp. [h]), the stabilizer (resp. the orbit) for the conjugacy action.

We claim the following chain of inequalities and equalities:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \#H^1(G,\Gamma ) \le \#(\Gamma /\sigma \text {-conj})= \#(\Gamma /\Gamma )^{G}\le \#(\Gamma /\Gamma ).$$\end{document}#H1(G,Γ)≤#(Γ/σ-conj)=#(Γ/Γ)G≤#(Γ/Γ).Since the first and the last inequalities follow from the inclusions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(G,\Gamma )\subset (\Gamma /\sigma \text {-conj})$$\end{document}H1(G,Γ)⊂(Γ/σ-conj) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma /\Gamma )^{G}\subset \Gamma /\Gamma $$\end{document}(Γ/Γ)G⊂Γ/Γ, we just need to prove the middle equality.

By the orbit-stabilizer theorem, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{g \in [g]_\sigma } \# {Stab}_\sigma (g) = \#\Gamma $$\end{document}∑g∈[g]σ#Stabσ(g)=#Γ. Therefore,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{g \in \Gamma } \# \text {Stab}_\sigma (g) = \sum _{[g]_\sigma \in \Gamma /\sigma \text {-}{conj}}\sum _{g \in [g]}\#\text {Stab}_\sigma (g) = \#\Gamma \cdot \# ( \Gamma /\sigma \text {-}{conj}). $$\end{document}∑g∈Γ#Stabσ(g)=∑[g]σ∈Γ/σ-conj∑g∈[g]#Stabσ(g)=#Γ·#(Γ/σ-conj).Moreover,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{g \in \Gamma } \# \text {Stab}_\sigma (g)&= \sum _{g \in \Gamma } \#\left\{ h \in \Gamma \mid hg\sigma (h)^{-1} = g \right\} = \sum _{h \in \Gamma } \#\left\{ g \in \Gamma \mid \sigma (h) = g^{-1}hg \right\} \\&= \sum _{[h] \in (\Gamma /\Gamma )^G} \sum _{h \in [h]} \# \left\{ g \in \Gamma \mid \sigma (h) = g^{-1}hg \right\} = \sum _{[h] \in (\Gamma /\Gamma )^G} \sum _{h \in [h]} \# \text {Stab}(h) \\&= \sum _{[h] \in (\Gamma /\Gamma )^G} \# \Gamma = \# (\Gamma /\Gamma )^G \cdot \# \Gamma , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑g∈Γ#Stabσ(g)=∑g∈Γ#h∈Γ∣hgσ(h)-1=g=∑h∈Γ#g∈Γ∣σ(h)=g-1hg=∑[h]∈(Γ/Γ)G∑h∈[h]#g∈Γ∣σ(h)=g-1hg=∑[h]∈(Γ/Γ)G∑h∈[h]#Stab(h)=∑[h]∈(Γ/Γ)G#Γ=#(Γ/Γ)G·#Γ,where the penultimate equality follows again from the orbit-stabilizer theorem (now applied to the action of Γ Γ on Γ Γ by conjugation). This concludes the proof. □ □

Smith–Thom inequality for groupoid cohomology

In the previous sections, we examined the topological spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {\mathscr {X}}^G \right| $$\end{document}XG and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| {\mathscr {X}} \right| $$\end{document}X associated to a topological G-groupoid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}} = [R \rightrightarrows U]$$\end{document}X=[R⇉U], and proposed Conjecture 1.6.

In a complementary direction, it is natural to compare the groupoid cohomology of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}^G$$\end{document}XG with the groupoid cohomology of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}X, as another route to generalizing the Smith–Thom inequality. More precisely, let us suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}X is an étale groupoid—that is, the source and target maps s, t :R → Us,t:R→U are local homeomorphisms—equipped with an involution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :{\mathscr {X}} \rightarrow {\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}σ:X→X. Then, as shown in Lemma 2.11, the fixed-point groupoid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}^G$$\end{document}XG is also étale. In this context, one may consider the groupoid cohomology \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*_{{grp}}$$\end{document}Hgrp∗ of both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}^G$$\end{document}XG (cf. [6, Section 2.1]).

Remark that the cohomology group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}}, \mathbb {Z}/2)$$\end{document}Hgrp∗(X,Z/2) is not finite dimensional in general, hence it does not make sense to compare the dimension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}}^G, \mathbb {Z}/2)$$\end{document}Hgrp∗(XG,Z/2) with the dimension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ H^*_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}}, \mathbb {Z}/2)$$\end{document}Hgrp∗(X,Z/2). Instead, one can ask the following.

Question 5.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {X}} = [R \rightrightarrows U], \sigma :{\mathscr {X}} \rightarrow {\mathscr {X}})$$\end{document}(X=[R⇉U],σ:X→X) be an étale G-groupoid. Is there a constant C > 0C>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \frac{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}}^G,\mathbb {Z}/2)}{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}},\mathbb {Z}/2)} \le C \quad \quad \text {for each } i \ge 0 \text {?} $$\end{document}dimHgrp≤i(XG,Z/2)dimHgrp≤i(X,Z/2)≤Cfor eachi≥0?If such a bound exists, can it be made independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}X (and, in particular, of the involution σ σ)?

Is there a constant C > 0C>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \frac{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}}^G,\mathbb {Z}/2)}{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}},\mathbb {Z}/2)} \le C \quad \quad \text {for each } i \ge 0 \text {?} $$\end{document}dimHgrp≤i(XG,Z/2)dimHgrp≤i(X,Z/2)≤Cfor eachi≥0?

Is there a constant C > 0C>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \frac{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}}^G,\mathbb {Z}/2)}{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}},\mathbb {Z}/2)} \le C \quad \quad \text {for each } i \ge 0 \text {?} $$\end{document}dimHgrp≤i(XG,Z/2)dimHgrp≤i(X,Z/2)≤Cfor eachi≥0?

If such a bound exists, can it be made independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}X (and, in particular, of the involution σ σ)?

If such a bound exists, can it be made independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}X (and, in particular, of the involution σ σ)?

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}}$$\end{document}X is a topological orbifold arising as the quotient of a topological space X by the action of a finite group Γ Γ, then by [27, Section 1.3], we have an isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ H^i_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}} ,\mathbb {Z}/2) \simeq H^i_\Gamma (X ,\mathbb {Z}/2), $$\end{document}Hgrpi(X,Z/2)≃HΓi(X,Z/2), where the right-hand side denotes the Γ Γ-equivariant cohomology of X. Moreover, by Example 2.6, if Γ Γ is a finite group with an involution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :\Gamma \rightarrow \Gamma $$\end{document}σ:Γ→Γ, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}} = B\Gamma = [\Gamma \rightrightarrows \textrm{pt}]$$\end{document}X=BΓ=[Γ⇉pt] is the classifying groupoid of Γ Γ, then there is a natural isomorphism of topological groupoids \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\mathscr {X}}^G \cong \coprod _{[\gamma ]} B(\Gamma ^{\sigma _\gamma })$$\end{document}XG≅∐[γ]B(Γσγ), where [γ ][γ] ranges over the elements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(G, \Gamma )$$\end{document}H1(G,Γ), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B(\Gamma ^{\sigma _\gamma }) = [\Gamma ^{\sigma _\gamma } \rightrightarrows {pt}]$$\end{document}B(Γσγ)=[Γσγ⇉pt], and where, for an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Gamma $$\end{document}γ∈Γ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \sigma (\gamma ) = e$$\end{document}γσ(γ)=e, the subgroup \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma ^{\sigma _\gamma }\subset \Gamma $$\end{document}Γσγ⊂Γ consists of those \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in \Gamma $$\end{document}g∈Γ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \sigma (g)=g\gamma $$\end{document}γσ(g)=gγ.

Proposition 5.3

Question 5.2.1 has a positive answer when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}} = B\Gamma $$\end{document}X=BΓ for a finite G-group Γ Γ.

Proof

By [30, Corollary 2.2], the cohomology ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*(\Gamma , \mathbb {Z}/2)$$\end{document}H∗(Γ,Z/2) is a finitely generated graded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}_2$$\end{document}F2-algebra. Consider the associated Poincaré series defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P_\Gamma (t) :=\sum _{i = 0}^\infty \dim _{\mathbb {F}_2} H^i(\Gamma , \mathbb {Z}/2) \cdot t^i \in \mathbb {Z}[[t]]. $$\end{document}PΓ(t):=∑i=0∞dimF2Hi(Γ,Z/2)·ti∈Z[[t]].By a result of Venkov (see [30, Proposition 2.5]), one has that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_H(t)$$\end{document}PH(t) is a rational function for any finite group H. For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Gamma $$\end{document}γ∈Γ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \sigma (\gamma ) = e$$\end{document}γσ(γ)=e, we obtain a rational function9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_{\Gamma , \gamma }(t) :=\frac{P_{\Gamma ^{\sigma _\gamma }}(t)}{P_\Gamma (t)} \in \mathbb {Q}(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PΓ,γ(t):=PΓσγ(t)PΓ(t)∈Q(t).Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma ^{\sigma _{\gamma }}\subset \Gamma $$\end{document}Γσγ⊂Γ is a subgroup, it follows from (see [30, Proposition 2.5, Theorem 7.7]), that the rational function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\Gamma , \gamma }(t)$$\end{document}PΓ,γ(t) has no pole at t = 1t=1, and thus the evaluation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\Gamma , \gamma }(1) \in \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}PΓ,γ(1)∈Q is well-defined. Hence, the proposition follows from Lemma 5.4 below. □ □

Lemma 5.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(t) = \sum _{i \ge 0} a_i t^i$$\end{document}f(t)=∑i≥0aiti and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(t) = \sum _{i \ge 0}g(t)$$\end{document}g(t)=∑i≥0g(t) be power series with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_i, b_j \in \mathbb {Q}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}ai,bj∈Q≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0\ne 0$$\end{document}a0≠0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0 \ne 0$$\end{document}b0≠0. For N ≥ 0N≥0, define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_N = (\sum _{i = 0}^Na_i)/(\sum _{i = 0}^N b_i) \in \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}CN=(∑i=0Nai)/(∑i=0Nbi)∈Q>0. Assume that f(t) and g(t) are rational functions. Then the quotient h(t) = f(t)/g(t)h(t)=f(t)/g(t) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(1) = \lim _{N \rightarrow \infty } C_N$$\end{document}h(1)=limN→∞CN provided that h(t) has no pole at t =1t=1.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f(t), g(t) \in \mathbb {Q}(t) $$\end{document}f(t),g(t)∈Q(t), we can write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f(t) = \frac{P(t)}{(1 - t)^r Q(t)}$$\end{document}f(t)=P(t)(1-t)rQ(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(t) = \frac{R(t)}{(1 - t)^s S(t)}$$\end{document}g(t)=R(t)(1-t)sS(t) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P(t), Q(t), R(t), S(t) \in \mathbb {Q}[t] $$\end{document}P(t),Q(t),R(t),S(t)∈Q[t], and Q(1), S(1) ≠ 0 Q(1),S(1)≠0. The integers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ s \ge r \ge 0 $$\end{document}s≥r≥0 are the pole orders at t = 1 t=1. From [12, Theorem VI.1, p. 381], we know that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ a_n \sim \frac{P(1)}{Q(1)} \cdot \frac{n^{r - 1}}{(r-1)!}$$\end{document}an∼P(1)Q(1)·nr-1(r-1)! hence, by Faulhaber’s formula, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_N :=\sum _{n=0}^N a_n \sim \frac{P(1)}{Q(1)} \cdot \frac{N^r}{r!}$$\end{document}AN:=∑n=0Nan∼P(1)Q(1)·Nrr!. Likewise, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_N :=\sum _{n=0}^N b_n \sim \frac{R(1)}{S(1)} \cdot \frac{N^s}{s!}$$\end{document}BN:=∑n=0Nbn∼R(1)S(1)·Nss! so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{A_N}{B_N} \sim \frac{P(1) S(1)}{Q(1) R(1)} \cdot \frac{(s-1)!}{(r-1)!}\cdot N^{r-s} \sim \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0 & \text { if } s>r \\ \frac{P(1) S(1)}{Q(1) R(1)} & \text { if } r=s . \end{array}\right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ANBN∼P(1)S(1)Q(1)R(1)·(s-1)!(r-1)!·Nr-s∼0ifs>rP(1)S(1)Q(1)R(1)ifr=s.In both cases, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_N/B_N\sim h(1)$$\end{document}AN/BN∼h(1), proving the lemma. □ □

In fact, the proof of Proposition 5.3 shows something more precise. Let Γ Γ be a finite group and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :\Gamma \rightarrow \Gamma $$\end{document}σ:Γ→Γ be an involution. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Gamma $$\end{document}γ∈Γ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \sigma (\gamma )=e$$\end{document}γσ(γ)=e, define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\Gamma ,\gamma }(t) \in \mathbb {Q}(t)$$\end{document}PΓ,γ(t)∈Q(t) as in (9). Heuristically, the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\Gamma , \gamma }(1) \in \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}PΓ,γ(1)∈Q reflects the ratio between the total mod 2 Betti numbers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B(\Gamma ^{\sigma _\gamma })$$\end{document}B(Γσγ) and BΓ BΓ.

Corollary 5.5

Let Γ Γ be a finite G-group and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {X}} = B\Gamma $$\end{document}X=BΓ. Then, we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varinjlim _{i \rightarrow \infty } \left( \frac{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}}^G,\mathbb {Z}/2)}{\dim H^{\le i}_{{grp}}({\mathscr {X}},\mathbb {Z}/2)}\right) = \sum _{[\gamma ] \in H^1(G,\Gamma )}\varinjlim _{i \rightarrow \infty } \left( \frac{\dim H^{\le i}(\Gamma ^{\sigma _\gamma },\mathbb {Z}/2)}{\dim H^{\le i}(\Gamma ,\mathbb {Z}/2)}\right) = \sum _{[\gamma ] \in H^1(G,\Gamma )} P_{\Gamma ,\gamma }(1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lim→i→∞dimHgrp≤i(XG,Z/2)dimHgrp≤i(X,Z/2)=∑[γ]∈H1(G,Γ)lim→i→∞dimH≤i(Γσγ,Z/2)dimH≤i(Γ,Z/2)=∑[γ]∈H1(G,Γ)PΓ,γ(1).

This motivates the following weaker version of Question 5.2.2.

Question 5.6

Does there exist a constant C > 0C>0 such that for every finite group Γ Γ and involution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :\Gamma \rightarrow \Gamma $$\end{document}σ:Γ→Γ, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{[\gamma ] \in H^1(G, \Gamma )} P_{\Gamma ,\gamma }(1) \le C$$\end{document}∑[γ]∈H1(G,Γ)PΓ,γ(1)≤C?

A positive answer to Question 5.6 would provide a stepping stone toward a general positive answer to Question 5.2.2. However, even this zero-dimensional case is already quite subtle. For instance, the following example shows that one cannot take C = 1C=1.

Example 5.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {S}}_4$$\end{document}S4 denote the symmetric group on four letters, equipped with the trivial G-action. The elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma _1 :=e, \gamma _2 :=(12)$$\end{document}γ1:=e,γ2:=(12) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma _3 :=(12)(34) $$\end{document}γ3:=(12)(34) form a complete set of representatives for the equivalence classes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(G, {\mathfrak {S}}_4)$$\end{document}H1(G,S4). In particular, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \# H^1(G, {\mathfrak {S}}_4) = 3. $$\end{document}#H1(G,S4)=3. Moreover, the fixed-point subgroups attached to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1, \gamma _2$$\end{document}γ1,γ2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3$$\end{document}γ3 are the subgroups \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {S}}_4^{\gamma _1} = {\mathfrak {S}}_4$$\end{document}S41=S4, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {S}}_4^{\gamma _2} = \{e, (12), (34), (12)(34)\} \simeq \mathbb {Z}/2 \times \mathbb {Z}/2$$\end{document}S42={e,(12),(34),(12)(34)}≃Z/2×Z/2, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\mathfrak {S}}_4^{\gamma _3} = \{e, (12), (34), (12)(34), (13)(24), (14)(23), (1423), (1324)\} \simeq D_8$$\end{document}S43={e,(12),(34),(12)(34),(13)(24),(14)(23),(1423),(1324)}≃D8, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_8$$\end{document}D8 denotes the dihedral group of order 8. From [28, Theorem 4.1] and [19, Theorem 5.5], one computes the corresponding Poincaré series:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P_{\mathbb {Z}/2 \times \mathbb {Z}/2}(t) = \frac{1}{(1 - t)^2}, \quad P_{D_8}(t) = \frac{1}{(1 - t)^2}, \quad P_{{\mathfrak {S}}_4}(t) = \frac{1 + t^2}{(1 - t)^2(1 + t + t^2)}. $$\end{document}PZ/2×Z/2(t)=1(1-t)2,PD8(t)=1(1-t)2,PS4(t)=1+t2(1-t)2(1+t+t2).Evaluating the associated ratios at t = 1t=1, we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P_{{\mathfrak {S}}_4, \gamma _1}(1) = 1$$\end{document}PS4,γ1(1)=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{{\mathfrak {S}}_4, \gamma _2}(1) = P_{{\mathfrak {S}}_4, \gamma _3}(1) = 3/2$$\end{document}PS4,γ2(1)=PS4,γ3(1)=3/2. Consequently, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{[\gamma ] \in H^1(G, {\mathfrak {S}}_4)} P_{{\mathfrak {S}}_4,\gamma }(1) = 1 + \tfrac{3}{2} + \tfrac{3}{2} = 4. $$\end{document}∑[γ]∈H1(G,S4)PS4,γ(1)=1+32+32=4.
